Don't Let Conflict Erode Your Relationship

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 10, 2026 | Reviewed by Michelle Quirk

Couples can argue over even the slightest difference of opinion. It can be as simple as preferring different kinds of breakfast cereals. You want the healthy organic variety, and your partner wants the sugar-coated flakes he had as a child. This trivial dispute often escalates until, in the worst-case scenario, you’re ready to break up.

How did things get so out of control? If you love someone, you should be able to handle some daily bickering. Relationship researchers often point out ways that conflicts become dysfunctional. In the newest approach, focus is placed on emotional regulation as the key to neutralizing some of these unpleasant interactions.

In a new approach to understanding relationship difficulties, University of Quebec’s Aurélie Thuot-Jolicoeur and colleagues (2026) posed a conflict-ridden scenario at the outset of their paper. A couple argues over how to spend some of their time while on a Parisian vacation. But instead of luxuriating in the lovely scenery and enjoying all that Paris has to offer, they began to quibble over whether to see a museum on one of their precious days in the French capital. One partner excitedly suggested this activity, but the other shot her down, claiming to be too tired. Suspecting this was an excuse for disinterest, the protagonist in this story becomes distressed. Over the next few days, the memory popped up unexpectedly, creating renewed emotional wounds.

In posing the alternative to this unpleasant outcome, the authors suggest that the distressed partner could acknowledge feeling upset and then ask why the partner wasn’t interested. Empathizing with the partner rather than becoming upset could enhance the relationship as they “form a more understanding and insightful attitude”

If you’ve ever been in a situation such as this (maybe not in Paris…), you can undoubtedly feel some empathy for each relationship partner as the original scenario unfolded. To get from scenario one to scenario two, the authors suggest, requires that you engage your emotion regulation strategies. To do so, they propose, involves the motivational approach known as Self-Determination Theory (SDT). According to SDT, instead of avoiding conflict or reacting immaturely by sulking or yelling, you are better off using integrative regulation, in which you allow yourself to notice and ultimately accept your emotions, even the unpleasant ones. You can now enter into a conflict constructively as you seek to learn from the conflict about your own emotions and those of your partner.

Based on SDT, the U. Quebec authors proposed that it’s not conflict alone that causes problems; it’s also the tendency to ruminate unproductively over a past disagreement with your partner. The memory of an event, they suggest, can be just as important as the event itself, especially if it fails to be resolved or brings up unpleasant emotions. You need to be able to control your emotions when those memories pop up in much the same way you do when encountering the situation in the first place.

To test this idea, Thuot-Jolicoeur et al. asked participants to describe a memory of a significant conflict they experienced with a partner. To measure emotion regulation from the SDT perspective, they asked participants to rate items tapping dysregulation (“When I feel negative emotions, I can’t concentrate on the other things I have to do”) and integrative regulation (“On occasions, feeling negative emotions helped me to understand something about the situation I was in”). These were part of the dispositional emotion regulation questions. Separately, the authors rated the extent to which these strategies were used in the recalled event itself.

The conflicts couples brought into the study included these three themes: (1) sexuality and intimacy; (2) needs, values, and interpersonal differences; and (3) practical conflicts. Relationship quality was the outcome of the analyses using emotion regulation of each partner as predictors.

As hypothesized, when partners used integrative regulation when recalling a conflict, their relationship satisfaction was higher than when they used dysfunctional regulation. Over time, the authors proposed, if the recall of a conflicted memory prompts renewed use of dysregulated strategies (hostility, withdrawal, or rumination), the negative effects will only increase exponentially, driving the couple members further apart. If, instead, couples put the conflict in the rearview mirror through integrative regulation, they can move toward greater closeness and understanding.

Knowing that the dysregulated recall of a troubling event with your partner can be as detrimental to your relationship as the event itself can be helpful as you and your partner try to move past the difficult emotions. As in the Parisian vacation example, the partner who felt hurt and dismissed could use these feelings to gain important self-insights. Maybe the partner truly was tired and needed time to rest and refresh. This doesn’t mean that the partner had somehow fallen out of love or was otherwise ready to move on from the relationship.

Reworking the recall of an event that caused distress can help to nip your feelings of being hurt and rejected in the bud. Furthermore, as you try to move on, it can also be helpful to isolate the event itself as the cause of your distress rather than generalize to everything being wrong in the entire relationship. Sharing your feelings in a nonthreatening or non-accusatory way can get you and your partner onto a path toward greater understanding.

To sum up, relationships are never 100 percent smooth going, but zeroing in on the conflicts—and their memory—that shape your relationship can help you move from dysfunction to emotional acceptance.

Thuot-Jolicoeur, A., Mérette, V. S., Lapointe, V. A., Carbonneau, N., & Philippe, F. L. (2026). Emotion regulation in couples’ conflict memories: A dyadic examination of relationship quality. Journal of Family Psychology. Advance online publication. https://doi.org/10.1037/fam0001555

Christina Haack Says She Paid Off Her College Debt by ‘Hitting It Big’ on a Las Vegas Slot Machine

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Real estate investor Christina Haack doesn’t mind proving people wrong, even if that includes her own mom.

The “Flip or Flop” shared the unlikely story of her student loan payoff, after experiencing a “full circle” moment during a recent trip to Las Vegas.

In an Oct. 29 Instagram post, the HGTV personality, 43, shared a collection of photos where she’s posed in front of a slot machine while enjoying a glass of her own brand of wine, Clé Cachée.

“Really come full circle since age 21,” she explained in the caption before adding that she “paid off” her “college debt by hitting it big” on a slot machine called “top dollar.”

Haack was proud to close the loop “22 years later,” by getting to enjoy her own champagne while playing her “fav” machine. She called the experience “priceless,” and joked: “My mom always told me no one actually wins in Las Vegas. Turns out mama’s not always right.”

Her boyfriend, Chris Larocca, left a cheeky comment under the post, writing, “Maybe you have a good luck charm now…” to which she replied, “Most definitely.”

Haack has previously shared that when she was attending San Diego State, she “never thought about being on TV” and instead thought she “wanted to be a sports agent like Jerry Maguire.”

Eventually she realized she’d rather get her real estate license, and explained that she “got started in real estate at 21”—the same year she won big at a casino.

While Haack’s previous financial win may have been the luck of the draw, it’s safe to say her family is set up for success in a much more intentional way these days.

The mom-of-three recently made a splash online when she, her ex-husband Tarek El Moussa, and his new wife, Heather El Moussa, gifted daughter, Taylor El Moussa, a very expensive vehicle for her 16th birthday.

The close-knit crew of co-parents splurged on a $150,000 BMW X5, a lavish gift they said was “well deserved.”

“It’s such a beauty. I’m so happy for her,” Christina said in an Aug. 13 Instagram Reel that showcased the moment the teen was surprised with the new car, which had an enormous pink bow on it.

Heather wrote that the trio had worked together to surprise Taylor, and even joked that she was unsure if the car was more impressive, or if “the fact that three of us actually pulled this off” was a more jaw-dropping feat.

Tarek later shared more details about the purchase, explaining that the family had “good reason” to purchase the luxury vehicle during an appearance on the “Money Rehab” podcast with Nicole Lapin.

“She’s got a 4.2 GPA. She was MVP of her freshman soccer team. She’s an absolutely fantastic kid, and she didn’t just get it—she actually earned it,” he said. “If you ask me, she deserved it because she worked really, really hard.”

Noting that the car was also selected for its host of safety features, Tarek added that the vehicle was mostly his choice, as Taylor would have probably preferred a much-less-expensive Ford Bronco.

“Here’s the truth. She was flying in private jets when she was like, 1 year old,” he said. “So this kid has grown up around things. When I grew up, I didn’t have anything, right? So I’m obsessed with things because I never had things. She’s always had things.”

Staying firm in his belief that the gift was a “reward for all the work she’s done,” Tarek revealed that the family has also set her up for success financially in addition to showering her with pricey gifts.

Tarek noted that he has snapped up stocks and investment real estate for his teen daughter, and said that “if she were going to be a spoiled brat, she probably wouldn’t have got the 4.2 GPA, she probably wouldn’t have been MVP of her soccer team, and she probably wouldn’t have done the extra coaching.”

Brandon Aiyuk likely will be cut by 4:00 p.m. ET today

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A key fact has come into focus regarding the Brandon Aiyuk situation. And it points to a likely conclusion to the 49ers’ portion of the situation by 4:00 p.m. ET today.

Although it’s not clear how this happened (the Player Personnel Manual indicates that his 2025 salary of $1.17 million should have tolled into 2026 due to placement on the reserve/left squad list), official NFL records now show Aiyuk as having a base salary of $13.75 million for the balance of 2026. This means that, if he’s on the roster at the close of business on Tuesday, his salary for Week 10 vests — at $1.52 million.

They surely won’t be able to trade him at that number. And they surely don’t want to give him $1.52 million. Even more surely, coach Kyle Shanahan doesn’t want him in the building.

There’s still a story to be told as to the deal that was done to get the league to reinstate Aiyuk. The NFL and 49ers were putting the squeeze on Aiyuk to refund upwards of $13 million in previously-paid bonus money.

Our guess? Aiyuk wired a sizable payment to the 49ers (maybe $6 million or $7 million), the NFL reinstated him, and now Aiyuk will get his freedom.

The next question will become where he lands.

While Libby soft-launched the departure with some posts on Instagram, Andrei was a bit more explicit. He popped up in the comments and shared a goodbye in a way only he could. For better or worse, this departure is unapologetically filled with Andrei's signature attitude (via John Yates):

For the imbeciles that watched for 10 years and were not entertained for what I bring to the table, suck it. Go color your hair pink and go protest lol. I'm Shocked By How Much 90 Day Fiancé's Sarper Reportedly Made Doing Stand-Up Comedy

Pixel Tag tidbits: Surprise NFC, Find Hub setup, & Bellroy keychain cases

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

The Made by Google 2026 lineup is finally complete with tomorrow’s retail launch of the Pixel Tag. You can find our review here, while there are some additional details about the device since the August announcement.

The Pixel Tag is the first Find Hub tracker with NFC. Google did not mention this connectivity specification in August, with Near Field Communication letting you tap the tracker to the back of your phone to immediately open device settings. The NFC area on the Pixel Tag is below the circular battery door, which you hold down and twist counterclockwise to open.

On Android 17 QPR2, tapping immediately opens the details page. On QPR1, you first get a system notification that you need to click. NFC joins Bluetooth 6.0 with Channel Sounding and Ultra Wideband (UWB).

The Pixel Tag has a physical button on the rear side below the battery cover. Clicking twice plays a loud sound on your phone if both devices are nearby. Once found, you can tap the button once to silence, or turn it off from your phone. This feature can be disabled from the Pixel Tag’s device details page on a tracker-by-tracker basis.

Google does not have a first-party case for the Pixel Tag. Instead, it’s working with Made for Google partner Bellroy.

There are two models available from the Google Store, with the first made of silicone. The Bellroy Lite Case Duo costs $24.99 and comes in a pack of two: Mist and Shadow colorways. It wraps everything but the face of the Pixel Tag (and its speaker cutout), while giving you a hole in the corner and color-matching cord.

There’s also the Bellroy Leather Tag Case in Artichoke, Azalea, Black, Pewter, and Red Ochre for $29.99.

Australia's Cheapest New Car Is This $13,000 MG Hatchback, And It's Far From A Stripper

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Chinese cars have been beating down on long established performance and luxury car brands for a while now, all while costing a fraction of the price. From jumping over potholes to setting top speed records to beating Land Cruisers in off-road tests and even having emergency water travel capabilities, there are some mighty impressive pieces of kit across the Chinese car industry. What I'm most interested in, though, are the ultra-cheap economy cars that sell for the price of a used shitbox, and that's exactly what the MG3 offers to Australian car buyers. It's a compact hatchback with the face of a plecostomus, which isn't especially exciting, but its $13,020 USD price sure is.

It's no stripper, either. That bargain price includes features like Apple CarPlay and Android Auto, steering wheel controls, air conditioning, a digital driver's display, an auto-hold function, automatic power window switches, and adaptive cruise control with lane centering. It even has a standard feature that the new Toyota RAV4 doesn't even offer on its most expensive trim level that happens to be four times the price of the MG3: padded front and rear armrests. OK, so the MG3's actual MSRP in Australia is $13,890.85 USD at current exchange rates, but the fact that a dealer was even willing to discount it further is remarkable, too.

As you can see from CarSauce's review, the MG3 is woefully slow. Its 1.5-liter four-cylinder engine sends just 109 horsepower and 105 pound-feet of torque to the front wheels via a continuously variable transmission, which results in a leisurely 0-to-62 mph time of 12.57 seconds. It ain't quick, but it gets the job done, and again, it's a $13,000 car. The last car sold in the United States that was near the $13,000 mark was the diminutive Chevrolet Spark, which ran the 0-to-60 sprint in 10.8 seconds in Car and Driver's testing, so the MG3 isn't too far off that pace. Plus, looking beyond straight-line speed (or lack thereof), the reviewer even says it rides great and handles respectably, which are not always the case with bargain-basement cars.

Sadly the MG3's weak engine doesn't equate to exceptional fuel efficiency, with the presenter's real-world economy equating to about 36 mpg on the highway and more like 26 mpg around town. Those aren't abysmal numbers, just perhaps not as good as one might hope from such a weak engine in a tiny car.

Beyond the lower-than-low base price, the MG3's value proposition really comes into focus when you take into account its 10-year manufacturer warranty. For those who say, "why spend $13,000 on the cheapest new car available when you can buy a bigger, better used car for the same price?" Consider the idea of spending $13,000 on a car with a 10-year-long warranty versus spending that same amount on a used car with no warranty. The new car comes with an entire decade of mechanical coverage so the buyer doesn't have to worry about unexpected repair costs for those 10 years, whereas the used car could end up requiring costly repairs within the first year of ownership.

There are some quality concerns noted in the video, including infotainment software glitches and some apparent inconsistencies in the operation of those fancy automatic windows, but considering the price and that stellar warranty, I'd say the pros outweigh the cons in this case. For those who view their cars as another appliance, or those who simply want the financial security of not worrying about repair costs for a whole decade, the MG3 must be a pretty tempting option. Provided you live in Australia, of course — or Mexico, Europe, the Middle East, or other parts of the world where it's pretty damn cheap, too.

Democratic Sweep Puts Crypto Bill On a Cleaner Track

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Home » Regulations » Democratic Sweep Puts Crypto Bill On a Cleaner Track

Regaining the majority at the Senate has shuffled the cards for crypto legislation: will Democrats handle it better?

Democrats’ control of both chambers of Congress has raised fresh questions about how soon Washington can deliver a lasting framework for the digital asset industry.

The stalled CLARITY Act and related market-structure legislation now sit before a Congress that is expected to reopen the terms, priorities, and timing of any overhaul.

The CLARITY Act was meant to settle a core industry question: which federal regulator oversees different parts of the crypto market. Its path was already uncertain.

The new congressional balance adds further negotiation over consumer protections, disclosure rules, clearer exchange oversight, and the split of authority between agencies.

Democrats Want to Rewrite the CLARITY Act and Crypto Could Pay the PriceDemocrats are reportedly preparing a major rewrite of the CLARITY Act after their midterm victory.The proposed changes could give the SEC greater oversight of crypto markets. The language, highlighted by… pic.twitter.com/DepyDC7V2J

Democratic majorities are likely to place a crystal-clear emphasis on investor safeguards and enforcement tools before endorsing a broad framework favored by many digital-asset firms.

Quick legislation is not ruled out, but any significant bill that advances could look different and move more slowly than earlier expectations.

Recently, Federal agencies have continued examining registration pathways and rules for crypto trading platforms even as Congress deliberates.

Crypto firms therefore face an evolving patchwork of agency actions while lawmakers work toward a more permanent statutory settlement.

Industry legal observers now see comprehensive market-structure rules crucial for the CLARITY Act potentially slipping into 2027.

Bitcoin’s recent rebound and renewed inflows into spot Bitcoin ETFs show that institutional interest has held up despite the political uncertainty.

Those ETF flows have offered the market some support, yet they do not resolve the regulatory questions still facing exchanges, token issuers, and decentralized-finance projects.

Market participants can already gain exposure through regulated products while much of the underlying industry waits for clearer operating rules.

A longer legislative process would likely preserve that divide, favoring firms able to absorb compliance costs and adapt to agency guidance.

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: Damian Hardung (Maxton Hall) and Charlotte Hope (Game Of Thrones) are set to star in crime-thriller Human Remains, which Blue Fox Entertainment is launching for sales at the AFM.

Based on the novel by Elizabeth Haynes, which is inspired by real events, William Nunez (The Laureate) is due to direct from a screenplay he co-wrote with Haynes.

Principal photography is scheduled to begin on location in Kent and around London in February 2027, with additional casting in process.

The synopsis reads: “Annabel (Hope) is a police analyst in a quiet English town who begins to notice a disturbing pattern: people are dying alone, their bodies going undiscovered for weeks, and the number of deaths is rising every month. Colin (Hardung) is an alluring yet unassuming man who insinuates himself into the lives of the lonely and forgotten, leaving nothing behind. While Annabel’s colleagues dismiss the deaths as tragic but natural, she suspects a serial killer is at work—and that he is already selecting his next victim: her. As the body count rises and the trail grows cold, Annabel must convince a department unwilling to listen and catch a killer no one can see before he strikes again.” Watch on Deadline Related Stories News Female-Led Action-Thriller 'Topple' Launches With Carla Diaz, Maria Pedraza, Tamara Feldman & Graham McTavish Among Cast -- AFM News 'Andy & Orson': First Look At Louis Partridge & Anson Boon In Coming-Of-Age Movie As Filming Wraps & Further Cast Revealed -- AFM

Together, they create a dynamic that is electric and emotionally gripping. At a time when audiences are looking for elevated thrillers with real substance, Human Remains feels especially relevant. I’m thrilled to be partnering again with Blue Fox Entertainment, whose passion for bold, commercially engaging storytelling makes them the ideal home for this project.”

“There’s a huge appetite worldwide for crime thrillers rooted in great IP, and Human Remains delivers on every level. It’s smart, unsettling and grounded in the same territory as Mare of Easttown and Zodiac,” added Lisa Gutberlet, President of Blue Fox Entertainment. “Charlotte Hope and Damian Hardung are a dynamic pairing with real global appeal, and we’re excited to bring this story to audiences around the world.”

Charlotte Hope is represented by Verve Talent and Literary Agency as well as United Agents in the UK. . Damian Hardung is represented by Subtitle Talent.

The Children’s Place is closing stores, joins list of struggling retailers to file for bankruptcy in 2026

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

The Children’s Place (Nasdaq: PLCE), a popular children’s clothing retailer with around 500 locations, has announced it is filing for bankruptcy.

The news comes as the company has struggled for years with declining sales and higher costs—not to mention a falling stock price. Yet, for now at least, the company says it plans to keep operating if possible, while also noting that some of its stores will close. Here’s what you need to know.

What’s happened?

This week, The Children’s Place Inc. filed for Chapter 11 bankruptcy protection in the United States Bankruptcy Court for the District of New Jersey.

In the filing, the company listed assets of approximately $742.3 million and debts of approximately $881.3 million on the books.

The filing comes just months after the company reported its Q2 2026 financial results in mid-September. Those results, which covered the period up to August 1, showed significant sales declines. 

In Q2, The Children’s Place posted revenue of $241.8 million, an 18.9% decline from the same quarter a year earlier. Gross profits fell to $83.3 million from $101.3 million for the same quarter a year earlier. 

The company reported it had 514 stores open at the end of the quarter, an increase of 19 stores from the previous quarter. It also operates an online storefront.

At the time, the company said comparables weighed on the results, particularly because “last year’s [2025] strong back-to-school demand was uniquely elevated by customer demand ahead of anticipated tariff price increases, which skewed those financial results compared to this year’s.”

Announcing the bankruptcy this week, The Children’s Place says it intends to “pursue a value-maximizing sale transaction” with the goal of selling “substantially all of its assets.”

The retailer is now seeking bids from interested parties by December 9, and if it receives any, expects to sell to a new buyer by the end of December.

Are The Children’s Place stores closing?

Unfortunately, some of them will. Though The Children’s Place is putting on its best face and insisting it wants to see the retail chain continue operating under new ownership, it also says that, even if that happens, some stores will be closing.

The company states that it “has been evaluating its operational footprint to invest its resources in its strongest locations moving forward” and “has determined that certain store closures will be necessary during the chapter 11 process.”

However, as of the time of this writing, all The Children’s Place locations and its online store are still open. Currently, the company’s website displays a large banner advertising a “total inventory blowout” on all in-store and online items. It adds that all sales are final.

Where does The Children’s Place have stores?

As of the end of Q2, The Children’s Place said it had 514 locations. Those locations include stores in the United States, Canada, Bahrain, Israel, Mexico, Oman, Qatar, Saudi Arabia, UAE, and Venezuela.

The company so far has not disclosed which locations will close as part of the bankruptcy process.

In the United States, the largest number of The Children’s Place stores are located in California, with 50 locations. It also has a significant number of stores in Florida and New York.

PLCE stock craters in 2026

The Children’s Place was founded in 1969 and went public in 1997. In 2018, the company’s stock price hit an all-time high of more than $161 per share. But since about 2021, the company’s stock price has steadily declined, and in 2026 it has cratered.

In January, the company’s stock price hit a 2026 high of around $5.09 per share. But since the beginning of the year, its stock price has now declined more than 95%. 

As of the time of this writing, PLCE stock price is just 18 cents.

SudTirol, Zeroli convocato da Baldini: prenderà parte alle sfide dell'Italia Under 21

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Dopo aver dovuto rinunciare a Gabriele Calvani, Matteo Cichella (entrambi del Frosinone), Cher Ndour (Fiorentina) e Dominic Vavassori (Palermo), il tecnico dell'Italia Under 21 Silvio Baldini è corso ai ripari convocando il centrocampista del SudTirol Kevin Zeroli che in serata si unirà ai compagni nel ritiro di Tirrenia per preparare le gare amichevoli contro Francia e Belgio in programma venerdì 13 (alle 18.30 a Laval) e lunedì 16 (all’Armando Picchi di Livorno alle 18.15). Per il classe 2005 si tratta di un ritorno in azzurro dopo l'esordio di un anno fa contro il Montenegro nelle qualificazioni all'Europeo e la presenza da titolare lo scorso giugno contro l'Albania con Carmine Nunziata in panchina.

In precedenza il tecnico Baldini aveva chiamato anche altri due giocatori di Serie B come Mattia Mannini del Benevento e Simone D'Uffizi dell'Ascoli per far fronte alle diverse defezioni rispetto alla lista originale.

Portieri: Giovanni Daffara (Parma), Diego Mascardi (Torino), Lorenzo Palmisani (Frosinone), Massimo Pessina (Bologna) Difensori: Davide Bartesaghi (Milan), Fabio Chiarodia (Borussia M’Gladbach), Pietro Comuzzo (Torino), Eddy Kouadio (Monza), Emanuele Lulli (Roma), Filippo Mane (Borussia Dortmund), Brando Moruzzi (Avellino) Centrocampisti: Lorenzo Amatucci (Deportivo La Coruna), Tommaso Berti (Cremonese), Matteo Dagasso (Venezia), Giacomo Faticanti (Avellino), Luca Lipani (Sassuolo), Mattia Mannini (Benevento), Kevin Zeroli (SudTirol) Attaccanti: Alessio Cacciamani (Torino), Francesco Camarda (Milan), Luigi Cherubini (Benevento), Jeff Ekhator (Juventus), Seydou Fini (Frosinone), Samuele Inacio (Borussia Dortmund), Simone D'Uffizi (Ascoli)

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s affair with alleged Chinese spy: a timeline

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia co-founder CEO Jensen Huang has never been as flashy or controversy-prone as AI leaders like OpenAI’s Sam Altman or SpaceXAI’s Elon Musk. Now, the 63-year-old industry kingpin has admitted that he cheated on his wife of 40 years in an affair that could have ramifications beyond Huang’s personal life and potentially impact national security.

United States officials believe that Huang’s likely romantic interest, Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, is a Chinese spy who has been feeding information back to Beijing. (Alice Huang, a Chinese citizen, is of no relation to Jensen Huang.)

Megaspeed, a Singapore-based AI infrastructure company, had reportedly purchased $4.8 billion in Nvidia chips over the last two years. The U.S. and Singapore have both previously investigated Megaspeed over suspicions that the firm was illegally sharing chips with China.

The U.S. and China are embroiled in a fierce technological arms race to build the most advanced AI infrastructure, which could shape each country’s economic future, military prowess, and capacity for innovation. Nvidia is valuable in this competition as one of the world’s largest makers of AI chips, which are essential for accelerating AI development. But as an American company, Nvidia faces government restrictions on the sale of its most powerful chips to Chinese entities.

As the controversy unfolds, we are organizing the most relevant events into a reverse-chronological timeline to keep you up to date.

The Nvidia CEO pulled out of upcoming speaking engagements at the ElevenLabs Summit and Anthropic CEO Forum.

Alice Huang and her family reportedly built a network of companies in China and Singapore to acquire Nvidia chips, including founding a Chinese company called Shanghai Century Litong. Founded in 2023, it became a Nvidia preferred cloud partner giving it priority access to chips, the Information reported. The Info reported that Shanghai Century Litong. facilitated buying $7.5 billion worth of Nvidia’s chips, including banned chips.

Nvidia’s head lawyer, Tim Teter, intervened in a 2025 US government probe surrounding the suspected smuggling of AI chips by Megaspeed International, Bloomberg reported. Teter contacted Commerce Under Secretary Jeffrey Kessler asking questions about whether the Trump administration played a role in detaining Alice Huang.

In early 2025, Alice Huang and a business partner sought China-based investors for an Asian data center, promising they had no problem in securing GPUs, the Financial Times reported.

On Sunday night, President Trump weighed in on Huang’s controversy and showed support for the Nvidia CEO.

“I have not spoken to him about it. I just know he’s a fine person, but you never know what’s going on,” Trump said.

When asked if Huang should remain chief of Nvidia, Trump said, “I think so. Oh yeah, sure.”

Jensen Huang wrote in a letter to Nvidia employees — which was also published as a press release — that he cheated on his wife.

“I owe my family and all of you an apology,” Huang told his company. “I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry.”

Huang did not mention Alice Huang by name in the message, but linked his infidelity to “the national security concerns raised in news reports.”

“Nvidia’s Board of Directors has initiated an independent investigation. I support the investigation and will cooperate fully. I love our country and our company. I am committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America’s national security. I am proud and grateful to be an American,” Huang wrote.

Nvidia shares slid over 5% the following day.

Three Republican congressmen — Chairman John Moolenaar of the House Select Committee on China, Chairman Brian Mast of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, and Chairman Rick Crawford of the House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence — sent a request to Huang for a closed-door joint briefing with their three committees.

“Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the [People’s Republic of China]’s access to sensitive U.S. technology and information,” the chairmen wrote. “The issue of PRC infiltration and access to cutting-edge U.S. technology is core to the investigative work of our three committees this Congress. It is vital that Congress determines the extent to which U.S. national security has been placed at risk.”

U.S. officials had been investigating Alice Huang and Megaspeed for more than a year when the New York Times broke the story in late October that the two executives also had a close relationship.

A Nvidia spokesperson denied that this relationship was something out of the ordinary.

“Jensen’s interactions with Ms. Huang are not unusual or unexpected. Meetings with customers are part of a CEO’s job; Jensen talks with thousands of customer executives every year,” Nvidia spokesperson John Rizzo said. He added that “if the U.S. government believes that any person is a threat to American industry, it would let us know.”

U.S. officials circulated a secret memo among policymakers which warned that Megaspeed’s Alice Huang, who had a relationship with Jensen Huang, was a spy for China’s Ministry of State Security. This memo was not public knowledge until it was later reported by the New York Times.

Secretary of State Marco Rubio supervised the drafting of the memo.

Around the same time, per the New York Times’ report, U.S. President Donald Trump was briefed about intelligence findings that possible Chinese spy Alice Huang had formed a close relationship to Jensen Huang. Trump did not act on this information.

The Nvidia CEO, an ally of Trump, has repeatedly advocated for the U.S. government to let Nvidia expand its business in China.

Megaspeed was confirmed to be under investigation by both the U.S. and Singaporean governments in October 2025. As a customer of Nvidia, Megaspeed was suspected of helping Chinese companies get around American embargoes on chip exports.

Megaspeed, a neocloud that used Nvidia chips, had been renting computing power to Chinese internet giant Alibaba and other Chinese companies, the Times reported. A Commerce Department official who had visited Megaspeed’s data center in Malaysia in 2024 had found a hoard of Nvidia equipment that was still packed in boxes and the concern was that this equipment was was being directly shipped somewhere else, like China, rather than installed and sold as a cloud service.

Jensen Huang had been spotted socializing with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang as early as 2024 in Taiwan, where the Nvidia CEO was born.

These investigations into Megaspeed highlighted concerns about the degree to which Nvidia is able to track its chip exports. At the time, a Nvidia spokesperson told press that the company did not have “any reason to believe products had been diverted.”

“NVIDIA visited multiple Megaspeed sites yet again earlier this week and confirmed what we previously observed — Megaspeed is running a small commercial cloud, like many other companies throughout the world, as allowed by U.S. export control rules,” the Nvidia spokesperson said at the time.

PlayStation Plus has officially revealed the arrival of 10 new free games as part of the November 2026 updates to the catalog, and these are pretty huge games that you’ll want to check out. There have been rumors about the first free December game on PS Plus, but it’s well-known that the additions in November are far from over. Several free titles for November had been teased and were already known, but now, we’ve received official confirmation about which titles are arriving soon on Sony’s subscription service, and who can play which titles.

The official announcement confirms the rumors of a 10/10 SNES JRPG on PlayStation Plus in November: Dragon Quest 3 HD-2D, the 2024 remake of the 1988 Dragon Quest III. There are also some other massive titles arriving as part of the new November update to the catalog, like Death Stranding 2: On The Beach and Like a Dragon: Pirate Yakuza in Hawaii. The service is also getting Atomfall and the widely popular Hell Let Loose.

The list below highlights all 10 games arriving for free on PS Plus in November, with mentions of which Game Catalog it will be available on:

While these new additions are undoubtedly exciting for members, they are balancing a major loss that is coming soon. PlayStation Plus is losing several games on November 17, a total of 12. Among them are games like Red Dead Redemption 2 and Grand Theft Auto 5, which are certainly being removed as Rockstar Games gears up for the release of GTA 6 on November 19. This is the nature of the service’s updates, however, so it’s no surprise that new titles are replacing others that are being removed.

While the new games may not reach the sheer scale of the two Rockstar removals, these are major new additions to the catalog that shouldn’t be overlooked. Death Stranding 2’s availability, for example, is huge in its own right, especially considering how much of an upgrade it is in comparison to its prequel. Dragon Quest 3 HD-2D Remake is also a great title to have on your radar, especially considering you’ll now be able to play it for free on PlayStation Plus.

Ocarina of Time Remake Has Fans Freaking Out Over Possible Majora’s Mask Hints

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Covering the video games industry since 2017, with experience in news, articles, and reviews. Currently a writer at DualShockers and gaming magazines.

It hasn't even been a week since The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake launched, and fans are already wondering whether Majora's Mask, the beloved sequel's darker adventure, will receive the same treatment in the future – not that I'm one of them (okay, maybe I am!).

And apparently, people are already going to some pretty wild lengths to find clues hidden throughout the new remake. But hey, we love a good fan theory, especially when it involves getting excited about what's next for one of gaming's most iconic franchises. So, naturally, we decided to join in on the fun.

Basically, the most popular theory right now is that the latest version of Ocarina of Time already contains a few hints suggesting that a Majora's Mask remake is in development, potentially arriving much sooner than you might expect.

And the whole thing started in the very first area of the game: Kokiri Forest. Some players quickly noticed that Link's house has a slightly different shape in the remake. The most interesting part, though, is that some fans believe its new design might actually be a direct reference to Majora's Mask.

Thomas Mahler believes Nintendo missed an opportunity to improve the level design in the Zelda: Ocarina of Time Remake.

According to the theory, the house looks remarkably similar to the Deku Butler's Son, who happens to be at the center of one of the sequel's most heartbreaking stories. In case you need a little refresher, here's what happens: After completing the game's first dungeon, you can take part in a mini-game offered by the Deku Butler near the Deku Palace. Once you finish, he reveals that he used to play the same game with his son, who is no longer around.

What makes this even sadder is that, at the very beginning of the game, Link actually encounters the Deku Butler's Son by accident. He's the small tree-like figure found in the Lost Woods, whom Link comes across while chasing Skull Kid to retrieve his stolen ocarina.

The most popular theory right now is that the latest version of Ocarina of Time already contains a few hints suggesting that a Majora's Mask remake is in development, potentially arriving much sooner than you might expect.

Now, take a look at the comparison between Link's house in the Ocarina of Time remake and the Deku Butler's Son in the original Nintendo 64 version of Majora's Mask. And I'll let you draw your own conclusions:

But that's not all! Players have also found some rather interesting dialogue lines involving the Skull Kids in the Lost Woods.

For starters, there's the area with two Skull Kids who ask Child Link to play music with them (this only works as Child Link, by the way, since they will attack you as an adult). Besides obviously sharing their appearance with one of Majora's Mask's most recognizable characters, there's another curious detail: the opening notes of one of the songs they play are remarkably similar to the beginning of the Song of Healing, arguably the most iconic piece of music in the Nintendo 64 sequel.

The remake's dev team included younger folks, several of whom never played the original Ocarina of Time, who provided a much-needed modern view of things.

Moving on, there's another area in the Lost Woods where a lone Skull Kid sits atop a pillar. He's part of the Happy Mask Shop side quest, and if you offer him the correct mask (the Skull Mask), he'll respond with the following line: "I'd look tougher wearing that mask."

Interestingly, the dialogue box also highlights the words "that mask" in red. And, well, we all know which mask can really make a Skull Kid look intimidating, don't we? Here, take a look at these moments for yourself (again):

With all these little details adding up, fans are already starting to believe that Nintendo isn't simply considering a Majora's Mask remake but may actually have one in the works. The theory also suggests that the project could reuse assets from the new Ocarina of Time, while including even more direct connections between the two games.

Of course, it's entirely possible that people are just really, really hoping for a remake of the sequel – and honestly, I can't blame them! I share that excitement, after all. Still, it's worth remembering that Nintendo hasn't even hinted at the possibility of a new Majora's Mask remake, let alone confirmed that one is in development. So, for now, all we can do is keep our eyes open, enjoy the theories, and hope that this particular dream eventually becomes a reality.

Hyrule has never looked better as they look in The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time Remake, and critics seem to agree!

Another Drug Shows Potential for Functional Cures in Chronic Hepatitis B

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

— With highest dose of siRNA therapy, 19% achieved a functional cure 24 weeks after treatment ended

Reductions in hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg) were observed with a novel small interfering RNA (siRNA) therapy in patients with chronic hepatitis B virus (HBV) infection, according to a phase II study.

Among patients randomized to monotherapy with one of four doses of the siRNA HRS-5635, all 32 on the highest dose (400 mg) had HBsAg levels less than 100 IU/mL after 48 weeks of treatment, with 88% still at that level 24 weeks after treatment ended.

Among this high-dose group, 16% achieved a functional cure, with an HBsAg clearance level of less than 0.05 IU/mL after 48 weeks -- a percentage that rose to 19% by 24 weeks post-treatment, reported Xieer Liang, PhD, of Southern Medical University in Guangzhou, China, at the American Association for the Study of Liver Diseases annual meeting in Denver.

"A dose-dependent antiviral response was observed with HRS-5635," Liang said. "All HRS-5635 regimens were generally safe and well-tolerated."

An estimated 240 million people worldwide have chronic HBV infection, including about 640,000 U.S. adults. Chronic HBV can cause liver damage, cirrhosis, liver cancer, and death. Five antivirals and two immune modulators are approved in the U.S. to treat chronic HBV, with treatment regimens lasting from 6 months to over 1 year.

In a previous phase II trial, siRNA therapy with an immunomodulator reduced HBsAg in patients with chronic HBV infection. Last month, the FDA approved another nucleic acid-based therapeutic, the antisense oligonucleotide drug bepirovirsen (Hibsago), for patients with chronic HBV taking antiviral therapy based on results from the B-Well 1 and B-Well 2 studies. In those trials, 19% to 20% of patients with HBsAg levels between 100 and 3,000 IU/mL had a functional cure with bepirovirsen at week 72.

The current trial included patients with chronic HBV on stable nucleos(t)ide analogue therapy who had HBsAg levels over 100 and less than 3,000 IU/mL and HBV DNA levels less than 20 IU/mL. Patients could be hepatitis B envelope antigen (HBeAg) positive or negative, but they couldn't have cirrhosis. All patients received tenofovir amibufenamide during 48 weeks of HRS-5635 treatment.

Mean age ranged from 41.5 to 44.6 years in the four treatment groups, 59.4% to 76.7% were men, and mean HBsAg ranged from 1,058.2 IU/mL to 1,200.4 IU/mL. Most patients were HBeAg negative (65.6% to 73.3%).

The investigators defined functional cure as sustained HBV DNA suppression and HBsAg clearance of less than 0.05 IU/mL, with or without seroconversion, sustained for 24 weeks after treatment.

All four HRS-5635 doses achieved the study's primary endpoint of HBsAg reduction from baseline to week 12, with mean reductions of -1.26 log10 IU/mL in the 50-mg group, -1.90 log10 IU/mL in the 100-mg group, -1.99 log10 IU/mL in the 200-mg group, and -2.12 log10 IU/mL in the 400-mg group.

Among patients in the 400-mg group, 45% converted from HBeAg positive to HBeAg negative after 48 weeks of treatment, a percentage that slid to 27% at 24 weeks after treatment.

In patients receiving the 400-mg dose who were HBeAg negative and had HBsAg below 1,000 IU/mL at baseline, 33% reached the functional cure rate of HBsAg less than 0.05 IU/mL.

Across the four groups, 46.9% to 60% had a treatment-related adverse event. There were only two such events that were grade 3 or higher, and no serious adverse events related to treatment and no deaths occurred. Upper respiratory tract infections were the most common adverse event, occurring in 25% to 33.3% of patients in the four groups.

Alanine transaminase elevations occurred in up to 6.7% of patients during and after treatment, Liang said, but levels were generally stable during treatment, and no elevations reached a grade 3 level.

The follow-up period of 24 weeks after treatment ended was relatively short, Liang cautioned, but a longer-duration extension study is ongoing, along with a phase III trial.

14 traits show where Congress matches the U.S. public and where it falls short

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

A look at 14 traits of the 119th Congress, from age and prior jobs to religion and military service, and how they compare with the public.

Each new Congress tends to arrive with a headline about the record it just broke, and that habit makes it easy to miss how far the body still trails the people it governs. The gains themselves have been steady. For the eighth time running, a newly seated Congress has set a mark for racial and ethnic diversity, according to Pew Research Center. Even after that streak, the people who write federal law remain overwhelmingly white, with non-White lawmakers making up 26% of the 119th Congress. The country has changed faster than its legislature, and racial and ethnic minorities now account for 42% of the U.S. population. Breaking records and closing a gap are different achievements. On race, Congress has done the first many times over without yet doing the second.

The newer faces in Congress also come largely through one party. Pew Research Center reports that 84% of the Black, Hispanic, Asian American and Native American voting members of Congress are Democrats. Women are spread just as unevenly, filling 42% of the Democratic seats in Congress and 15% of the Republican ones, according to Pew Research Center. Together those figures place both kinds of diversity mostly on one side of the aisle. How closely Congress resembles the country therefore rises and falls with which party wins seats, and it doesn't depend only on slow, long-term change in who Americans are. That holds for more than race and gender, and the party behind each trait is worth checking.

Congressional Research Service set out to describe the demographic and career traits of lawmakers in the current Congress, from members' ages and the jobs they held before office to their religions and military service. Members' schooling, careers and earlier elected office are part of that picture too. Looking at those stages of a lawmaker's life changes the question from who wins seats to what kind of person usually becomes a lawmaker. Taken trait by trait, Congress is far from the public on some measures and has caught up on others, while on still others change is only starting at its edges.

Congress adds new faces mostly by replacing old ones. The current Congress has 541 members, according to the Congressional Research Service. Of those, 435 represent House districts, one member for each district, according to the Census Bureau. A newcomer can win one of those district seats only after the member who held it loses, retires or otherwise leaves. Each new diversity record is therefore a story about who left as well as who arrived, and each gain depends on an opening.

Very few Americans have ever held a seat in Congress. Since 1789, only 12,585 people have served, across more than two centuries of the nation's history. The lawmakers serving today are only the most recent members of that long, narrow line of officeholders. The profile on the slides that follow describes who holds a scarce set of seats at this moment. It does not describe a body that keeps opening its doors wider.

Who wins House seats depends on where district lines fall, and those lines stay in place across several elections. The districts used from the 118th through the 122nd Congress are based on 2020 Census results, according to the Census Bureau. House candidates therefore compete on the same maps for years at a time. A comparison between this Congress and those of earlier decades sets different maps against each other as well as different people.

Each trait ahead shows who won a limited number of seats on a given set of maps, not proof that Congress has grown more open.

Republicans run the House, but their majority is about as narrow as a working majority can be. The party holds 219 House seats to the Democrats' 212, according to the Congressional Research Service. NIH reports that control of a full House takes 218 of its 435 seats, so Republicans have very few seats to spare. Republican leaders need nearly every one of their members present and voting with them to pass a bill without Democratic help. A few absences or defections can defeat a measure that a larger majority would pass with ease. The membership of the House also changes during a session. Four House seats were vacant when the count was taken, which lowers the number of votes a majority needs while those seats stay empty. The party totals therefore describe one moment rather than a fixed balance, and the Republican margin stays thin either way.

Republicans won the Senate outright in 2024 instead of only narrowing a Democratic lead. Democrats controlled the chamber going into that election, NIH reports, and Republicans now hold 53 of its seats, according to the Congressional Research Service. Voters moved the Senate from one party to the other rather than leaving the same party in charge with a smaller margin. Together, the two chambers show how unevenly Republicans gained control of Congress. In the Senate they took power away from Democrats. In the House they kept power with only a narrow cushion.

The Senate shows how quickly voters can give a chamber to the other party, and the House shows how few seats would have to change for them to do it again.

Voters send new members to Washington every election, yet the House has not grown any younger. The average representative was 57.9 years old when the 119th Congress opened, exactly the same average as at the start of the 118th, according to the Congressional Research Service. A full round of departures and arrivals ended with the chamber at the same age. The Senate is older still, with an average senator age of 63.9. Most senators belong to the baby boom generation, Pew Research Center reports, and that majority helps account for how much older the Senate is than the House. In the Senate, a single generation still holds most of the seats.

Congress gets its younger members through newcomers, and this year's House newcomers were younger than the chamber they joined but older than the class before them. Newly elected representatives averaged 50.2 years, well below the House average, while the newcomers of the 118th Congress had averaged 47.8. Even with a freshman class well below the average, the House as a whole ended up no younger than before. This class also narrowed the age difference between newcomers and the chamber they joined.

In the House, age does not divide along party lines. Pew Research Center reports that House Democrats and House Republicans have nearly the same median age, so a younger House would have to come from members of both sides. The newest generation barely has a foothold. Rep. Maxwell Frost, a Florida Democrat, is the youngest member of the House and its only member of Generation Z.

One class of younger newcomers was not enough to lower the House average, and a younger Congress will take more than that.

Before they came to Congress, most members worked in politics itself, not in any one private profession. Some 305 representatives, making up 70% of the House, had served in an elected post somewhere in government, from city hall to the statehouse to Washington, according to the Congressional Research Service. A representative who had never run for office and won is the exception. Senators are even more likely to have held office before, and 82 of them, or 82%, had done so. Members bring many careers to Capitol Hill, but nearly all of them first learned to campaign and govern at a lower level.

Law is the profession most closely tied to Congress, and lawyers are much more common in the Senate than in the House. Most House members never practiced law. In the Senate, however, 47% of members hold law degrees and practiced law. Senators are more likely than representatives to have held earlier office and more likely to have worked as lawyers. Voters in Senate races therefore pick people with both credentials more often than voters in House races do. Even in the Senate, though, a career in law is a common route rather than the usual one. Earlier elected office is the experience that most members share.

Members who came from the private sector were far more likely to have run a company than to have represented workers. Before taking office, at least 135 representatives, or 31% of the House, had started, owned or managed a business. That group is as large as the group of House members who practiced law. Only a handful of members ever worked as union representatives, and all of them serve in the House. No senator has that background. Members with experience running a business hold far more seats than members who once spoke for workers, and both groups vote on wages and workplace rules.

On education, Congress comes almost entirely from the part of the country that finished college. Among House members, 96% hold a bachelor's degree or higher, according to the Congressional Research Service. Most American adults lack such a degree, with the share of those 25 and older who held a bachelor's or more at 38% in 2021, according to Census Bureau figures reported by Pew Research Center. No law requires a diploma to run for Congress, yet the people voters send to the House almost always have one. The college degree marks only where the gap with the public starts.

For most House members, college was not the end of their schooling. Some 66% of representatives hold an advanced degree beyond the bachelor's, which makes graduate school the usual path into the House. The typical representative has studied a full level beyond the college degree that most of their constituents never earned.

The House has grown more credentialed over the decades, not less. In the 89th Congress, which met in 1965-1966, 74% of House members had a bachelor's degree. Since then, House members have become nearly all college graduates. On this trait, the House has moved further from the public instead of closer to it.

Congress has become more racially diverse over the decades, but on schooling its members have grown more alike.

A sizable share of the House was new to the job when this Congress began. Some 135 representatives, or 30.6% of the chamber, had served two years or less, according to the Congressional Research Service. That large minority of the House came into this session with little time in Congress behind them.

Experience runs deeper in the Senate, where 45 senators served in the House first. That is close to half the chamber. For those senators, the move across the Capitol came after years of learning how Congress works. Members who start in the House, build experience there and carry it to the Senate keep a core of career politicians in place.

This Congress is about to lose a large share of its members, including some of the most familiar names in both chambers. TIME reports that at least 70 House members will not return, among them Pelosi, Hoyer and Nadler, and that McConnell and Durbin are retiring from the Senate. First-term lawmakers will fill the open House seats. Pelosi, Hoyer, Nadler, McConnell and Durbin held seats at the top of Congress, and they are leaving together in a single cycle rather than one at a time.

Not every departure is voluntary, and the forced ones show that long service does not guarantee a seat. Twelve incumbents in the House and Senate who sought reelection lost renomination, according to TIME. These members did not choose to step aside. Their own party picked someone else as its nominee. Time in office did not protect them in either chamber.

The steady, experienced core of senators who came up through the House belongs to this Congress alone, and the next one will likely be less experienced at the top as well as at the edges.

On religion, the Americans Congress most clearly leaves out are defined by belief: people who claim no religion. Almost every lawmaker names a faith, with 96% reporting a specific affiliation, according to the Congressional Research Service. Yet 28% of American adults claim no religious affiliation at all, according to Pew Research Center figures reported by EWTN News. Americans without a religion are a large minority of the adult public, but only a small share of lawmakers either decline to name a faith or report having none.

Most of the seats such Americans might hold go instead to members of one tradition. Protestants fill 55.1% of the seats in Congress but make up 40% of the public, according to Pew Research Center figures reported by EWTN News, so Congress has a Protestant majority that the country itself does not have. On belief, Congress is more religious and more Protestant than the people who elect it.

Lawmakers from Congress's minority faiths come almost entirely from one party. EWTN News reports that the Democratic caucus includes all of the lawmakers who identify as Muslim, Hindu, Buddhist, Unitarian Universalist or humanist. The Muslim and Hindu members form small groups, and all of them serve in the House. Republicans hold a small faith bloc of their own, because all nine Latter-day Saints in Congress are Republicans. Each of these groups of lawmakers comes from a single party, so its numbers can rise or fall with one election. Congress's religious variety therefore depends on which side wins seats more than on the beliefs of the country.

Women outnumber men among American adults, yet they hold well under a third of the seats in Congress. Pew Research Center reports that women make up 51% of Americans ages 25 and older, but they hold just 28.65% of the seats in Congress, according to the Congressional Research Service. That leaves a majority of the adult public with well short of half the seats where federal law is written.

The number of women in Congress stopped rising with the last election. Congress has 155 women today, the same number that served when the 118th Congress ended, so a full election cycle produced no net gain. With the count flat, women's share is not moving toward half of Congress, and it will stay at its current level until the count grows.

Voters mostly returned women to office. In the House, 104 of 110 women incumbents won reelection in 2024, Pew Research Center reports, and the number of women in Congress still did not change. Women did win seats for the first time, but their arrivals only balanced out the women who left Congress. Future gains therefore depend on women winning more seats than women give up, whether those seats open through retirements, runs for other offices or defeats.

Until women gain more seats than they lose, the gap between Congress and the women who make up most of the adult public will stay where it is.

On Black representation, Congress has nearly caught up with the country it governs. Black lawmakers hold 12.2% of the seats in Congress, according to the Congressional Research Service, close to the share of Black Americans in the nation as a whole. Jenn Hatfield of Pew Research Center reports that Black Americans make up 14% of the U.S. population and that Black lawmakers' share of Congress is on par with it. That is a real milestone, but it belongs to one group. Parity for Black Americans does not mean Congress has caught up with minority Americans as a whole.

Most Black lawmakers serve in the House. Just five Black lawmakers serve in the Senate, and every other Black member of Congress holds a House seat. The parity Black Americans have reached applies to Congress as a whole, and it rests mainly on members of the larger chamber. Black voters see far more people who share their background in one half of the Capitol than in the other.

Nearly all Black lawmakers are also Democrats. Republicans count four Black members in the House and one Black senator. Black parity in Congress therefore rests mostly on members of the Democratic caucus, and any shift in that caucus's size can move the number of Black lawmakers along with it.

The close match between Congress and Black America holds only as long as the party that sends most Black lawmakers to Washington keeps its seats.

Hispanic Americans hold far fewer seats in Congress than their numbers in the country would suggest, even in a session that set a record for diversity. Hispanic lawmakers hold 10.35% of the seats in Congress, according to the Congressional Research Service. Pew Research Center reports that Hispanics make up 20% of the U.S. population. The records Congress has set for racial diversity in recent sessions have not reached Hispanic Americans in proportion to their numbers. As a result, Hispanic Americans hold a share of Congress well short of their share of the public.

That shortfall does not mean nothing has changed, because Hispanic representation has grown a great deal from a very small start. In the 99th Congress, which sat in 1985 and 1986, there were 14 Hispanic members, all of them men serving in the House. At that point the community had no senator and no woman in either chamber. The growth since then is real progress. But it began so far behind that today's much larger group of Hispanic lawmakers still holds a share of Congress well below the community's share of the population.

Hispanic lawmakers are not drawn almost entirely from one party. Twelve House Republicans are Hispanic, which means that the number of Hispanic members does not rest on Democratic wins alone. Most Hispanic lawmakers are still Democrats, so the lean toward one side remains. Even so, Hispanic candidates win seats as Republicans as well as Democrats, so either party's victories can add Hispanic members to Congress.

Closing the gap with the population does not have to wait on one party's fortunes, because both parties send Hispanic lawmakers to Congress.

Asian and Pacific Islander lawmakers show a Congress that is still early in the process of changing. The group holds 24 seats, or 4.4% of Congress, according to the Congressional Research Service, which makes it a small presence among the people who write federal law. Even that small group is a large gain over the 99th Congress of 1985-1986, when Asian and Pacific Islander lawmakers held five House seats and two Senate seats. Its current size shows how far the group has come from a very small base, and it should not be read as a settled level.

Democrats hold most of those seats, which ties the group's presence in Congress closely to one party. Democrats account for 16 of the group's House members and Republicans for five, while all three senators in the group are Democrats. Asian and Pacific Islander representation in Congress therefore rests largely on the members of a single party.

Women in this group took their seats only after the mid-1980s. Every Asian and Pacific Islander member of the 99th Congress was a man, so each woman in the group today joined a delegation that had no women four decades ago. Asian and Pacific Islander women in Congress are therefore a recent development, with no earlier generation of women in the group before them.

The group's gains are real, and all of them have come within the past four decades.

Native Americans hold fewer seats than any other group in this profile of Congress, so few that a single election can change the picture. Congress had four Native American members as of August 2025, according to the Congressional Research Service. With a group that small, one arrival or one departure changes the total by a large share, so the count describes a moment rather than a settled level. In the Senate, where Native members have been rarest, the count is already out of date.

The Senate has been the hardest chamber for Native politicians to reach, and its record is only now starting to change. In the more than 230 years since the chamber was established, just four Native senators have held seats there, every one of them a man, according to The Associated Press. That record ends with Peggy Flanagan, who will be the first Native woman to serve in the Senate when she takes office.

Native lawmakers do not follow the pattern in which most of Congress's new diversity arrives through Democrats. Two of the three Native House members are Republicans and one is a Democrat, and the one sitting Native senator is also a Republican. Flanagan is a Democrat, according to The Associated Press. Once she is seated, the Senate will have Native members from both parties. For this group, a lawmaker's party does not predict whether that lawmaker adds to the diversity of Congress, and each group has to be judged on its own terms.

Native candidates are winning state offices as well as seats in Congress. New Mexico voters made Deb Haaland the first Native woman to win a governor's race in any state, according to The Associated Press. Her victory adds to the number of Native leaders who have held high office. "This is a seismic shift that is happening in our communities," said Jordan James Harvill, a Cherokee Nation citizen and strategist with Advance Native Political Leadership. Native members still hold 0.74% of the seats in Congress, so most of that shift has not yet reached the Capitol.

Congress is where this rise will show up last, and the low count reflects a group that is arriving late, not one that has reached its limit.

Congress is far less foreign-born than the country it governs. About 6.0% of members of the 119th Congress were born abroad, according to the Congressional Research Service. Across the country the share is much higher: In 2024, 15.4% of the people living in the U.S. had come from another country, according to Pew Research Center. Some members born abroad were born to American citizens, so even fewer lawmakers are immigrants than the foreign-born count suggests. Americans who moved here from another country have far fewer lawmakers who share that background than their numbers in the population would suggest.

Birthplace alone understates how close Congress is to the immigrant experience. Counting lawmakers whose parents came from abroad along with those who came themselves, at least 15% of Congress has immigrant roots, according to Pew Research Center. That group is far larger than the members born abroad. Many of these lawmakers are therefore the children of immigrants rather than immigrants themselves, born in the U.S. to parents who were not. When Congress takes up immigration policy, their family histories bring that experience into the debate as well.

Lawmakers with immigrant roots also sit mostly on one side of the aisle. Most of them are Democrats, and far fewer are Republicans, according to Pew Research Center. The number of lawmakers who know immigration from their own families therefore depends heavily on how many seats Democrats hold after each election.

The part of Congress that carries the immigrant story includes many children of immigrants and sits mostly in one party, and a single election can shrink it.

Most members of Congress have never served in the military. The current Congress has 98 veterans, or 18.1% of its members, according to the Congressional Research Service. That leaves most of the lawmakers who vote on war, the Pentagon budget and veterans' care without that experience of their own. The number has stopped falling, though. The 118th Congress also opened with 98 veterans, so the count was the same at the start of two Congresses in a row. On this trait, Congress is not setting new records. The question is whether its veterans can keep the number steady.

Veterans once made up a far larger share of Congress. In the 92nd Congress, which met in 1971 and 1972 during the Vietnam War, 73% of lawmakers were veterans. Many current members were alive to see a Congress in which military service was the norm. Since then, it has gone from a body in which most members knew military life firsthand to one in which most rely on the accounts of others. Lawmakers now decide whether to send troops into combat, and how to care for them when they come home, mostly without having served themselves.

Women make up a small part of the veterans in Congress. Nine women in Congress are veterans, and three of them saw combat. They are a small group among the 98 veterans, but their presence shows that the people who bring military experience to Congress do not all come from a single background.

Military service has become less common in Congress, and the members who still have it include women who served in combat.

See Bruce Springsteen Finally Play Western Stars Songs in Public

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

It’s been seven years since Bruce Springsteen released his acclaimed orchestral pop record Western Stars, but he’s never played any of the songs at a public concert despite all the touring he’s done in that time. That finally changed Monday evening when he performed his annual acoustic set at the Stand Up For Heroes benefit concert at New York City’s David Geffen Hall. The show included the Western Stars songs “Hello Sunshine” and “Moonlight Motel,” in addition to 2007’s “I’ll Work for You Love” and “Dancing in the Dark.”

In keeping with the grand tradition of Stand Up For Heroes, which is organized by the Bob Woodruff Foundation and has raised $123 million for veterans and their families over the past 20 years, Springsteen sprinkled dirty jokes throughout his set. (The show also included performances by Josh Johnson, Hasan Minhaj, the Resilient, Jon Stewart, and Alex Warren.)

“Guy goes into the library,” Springsteen deadpanned to the crowd. “Goes up to the librarian. He goes, ‘Ma’am, do you have a book on small penises?’ She goes, ‘I don’t think it’s in yet.’ ‘Yeah, that one.'”

He also revived the “voodoo penis” joke that he told at the 2018 Stand Up For Heroes event. It’s about a man who worries his wife will cheat on him while she’s out of town, so he buys her a “voodoo penis” that will magically go anywhere it’s asked.

“She realizes the husband didn’t tell her the word to turn off the voodoo penis,” Springsteen said. “After about 10 or 20 minutes, she’s had enough. She’s starting to get really nervous. ‘I gotta go to the hospital.’ She jumps in the car, hits the gas, she’s going 90 miles per hour in a 25-mile-per-hour zone. And she gets pulled over by a state trooper. He says, ‘License and registration.’ She says, ‘Officer, you don’t understand. A voodoo penis! A voodoo penis!’ He says, ‘Voodoo penis, my ass.'”

A joke about a sex shop worker selling a thermos to a woman requesting a plaid dildo was also familiar, though still delivered quite well. But the Western Stars songs were the clear highlight. In April 2019, Springsteen played the album in its entirety at a special concert in a barn on his New Jersey farm that was recorded for the Western Stars concert film and soundtrack. Last month, he sang “Stones,” “Hello Sunshine,” and “Moonlight Motel” during a private jam session at Danny Clinch’s Transparent Clinch Gallery in Asbury Park, New Jersey, on the same bill as Tom Morello, Dave Matthews, Jesse Malin, Daryl “DMC” McDaniels, and Taylor Momsen. But neither of those events was open to the public. Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

In 2025, Springsteen explained to Rolling Stone why he’d held back from playing songs from the album at his arena and stadium shows. “If I was going to do that, I’d go out on probably a smaller tour, where I might engage an orchestra and play those records specifically,” he said. “When I go out with the E Street Band, I go out to do a rock show. I want to come out, put the pedal to the metal for three hours, and exhaust you, myself, and anybody in the immediate 20-mile vicinity. And so, there’s certain things that mix and certain things that don’t mix for me, and that’s sort of the way I look at the work that I do with the band.” Trending Stories Watch Ariana Grande, Ben Stiller, Robert De Niro Take Over ‘The Tonight Show’ Paul McCartney and Ringo Starr to Reunite at Las Vegas Show in 2027 Pink Becomes First Solo Female Pop Artist to Headline the Sphere Judge Sets Steven Tyler Sex Assault Trial Date

Stand Up For Heroes was the final event on Springsteen’s public calendar for 2026. But he is working on two separate solo albums. “I did finish one, and it is done,” he told Rolling Stone in September. “I’m in the process of working on this other one, which, because of the nature of the material, felt like it should come out first. So that’s the situation I’m in. I’m trying to finish that up, and I’d like to release that first. And then this other record that I made, which is not time-relevant, it can come out at any time and it’ll work.”

He’s not sure exactly when he’ll tour again. “Well, we toured quite a bit, and we toured the previous spring,” he said. “We toured this spring. And these days that’s what I like to do. I would like to go out and do 20, 30 shows. I don’t imagine a 140-show E Street tour will happen again. It’s just time is too short right now. But I do imagine a good series of simply shorter tours where we touch different places at different times. And the band is playing so well. In my opinion, if you saw this show, you saw one of the best shows we’ve ever done. The band is playing so well that we have a happy life there, and that’ll continue.”

The Jets never got off the ground

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

In 2025, the New York Jets put together an unforgettable season worthy of eternal remembrance. When footballs were being passed through the air — a pretty common occurrence in the sport of football — this team of professional football players all of a sudden had all their talents stolen by the Monstars.

That is the most logical explanation for how an NFL team could play an entire (17-game!) season whilst neither producing a single player on offense who could top the meager total of 395 receiving yards NOR a single player on defense who could intercept a single one of the 515 passes that were thrown against 'em.

Here we'll explore the deep, dark, world that is the 2025 Jets and just how inconceivably difficult and unlikely pulling off such levels of ineptitude truly are. Much like Marge before a Halloween special, I must warn you that this gets very, very scary and those of you with young children may want to send them off to bed, lest they be exposed to Jets-itis. You've been appropriately warned.

Meta and IMAX sign deal to put an IMAX theatre in Meta’s VR Glasses

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

IMAX’s app will put Meta VR Glasses wearers in a virtual theatre, with some films in its exclusive Expanded Aspect Ratios. The $1,299.99 glasses launch in spring 2027.

IMAX and Meta have signed an agreement to make Meta’s VR Glasses the first VR device with IMAX Enhanced certification. IMAX will also bring an updated app to the glasses when they launch in spring 2027, the companies said on Tuesday.

The app will put viewers in a virtual theatre to watch IMAX Enhanced films and shows. Some titles will play in IMAX’s exclusive Expanded Aspect Ratios. IMAX says the aim is to bring audiences closer to the filmmaker’s vision, with optimised picture and sound.

“Meta is taking a bold new step in immersive entertainment, and we believe our brand and technology is a powerful differentiator for new and emerging devices like the Meta VR Glasses,” said Rich Gelfond, chief executive of IMAX.

Sony, Hisense, TCL and LG already make IMAX-certified TVs and audio devices. IMAX Enhanced content streams on Disney+, and on Sony Pictures Core through Amazon Prime Video.

Meta first said the glasses would be IMAX Enhanced certified when it unveiled them at its Connect event in September. The agreement with IMAX makes that official and adds the IMAX app.

The glasses go on sale in spring 2027 for $1,299.99. They sit above the $299 Meta Glasses that Meta launched in June.
