Mormon Wives' Jen Affleck Says She's "Not Doing the Best" Amid Divorce

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Jen Affleck, who stars on Hulu’s upcoming Nov. 12 series Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County, shared insight into her priorities amid her and Zac Affleck’s divorce.

Jen Affleck is swinging into a new chapter.

After filing for divorce from husband Zac Affleck, the Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County star shared insight into how she’s navigating her new normal.

“I'm not doing the best right now,” Jen—who is currently pregnant with her and Zac’s fourth baby—admitted to E! News in an exclusive interview. “I'm just taking one day at a time, and really just focusing on my kids. And obviously, the timing sucks, especially with me being pregnant.”

Nevertheless, she affirmed, “Ultimately, it was the best decision for us.”

And with that right decision bolstering her sails, Jen enjoyed sailing into the new horizon alongside her Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County costars Bobbi Althoff, Aspyn Ovard, Avery Woods, Madison Bontempo, McCall DaPron, Salomé Andrea, Ashleigh Pease and Jordan McClanahan.

For the Dancing With the Stars alum, the timing of filming Orange County made to an experience that was more freeing than her seasons on Secret Lives of Mormon Wives.

“What's funny is there was no reinventing this time,” the 27-year-old admitted. “I just showed up as me. I feel like for the past seven, eight years of my life, I've tried to mold myself into who I think people want to see, whether it be for my marriage or for TV or for the world. And this year was the first time that I really felt like I was reclaiming my identity and who I actually am.”

So, “going into the season, I just poured my heart into it,” she added. “Nothing was storyline; everything was real.”

That meant allowing the cameras to track her difficult chapter as it played out.

“I'm obviously not excited for people to see all the painful parts of the show,” she continued, “but I'm looking forward to people seeing the real, real me—and I guess I'm still trying to figure out who the real me is as well. So, we'll see that unfold in the future.”

As for how Jen showed up to The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County, fans can tune in for the premiere on Thursday, November 12 on Hulu and Hulu on Disney+.

While Jen has in large part remained publicly silent on her divorce—though she did share two videos from her and Zac’s relationship, highlighting his less-than-ideal behavior—Zac shared a five-minute video sharing insight into their split.

“This is something we've been talking about for a long time,” he said of their breakup in the Nov. 5 TikTok. “Jen came to me about a year ago, after Dancing With the Stars, and told me she was unhappy and wanted a divorce. And I basically begged her to stay, told her I’d work on things, fix things, become whoever she needed me to be."

It was during this time that Jen became pregnant with their fourth baby, who will join siblings Nora, 5, Luca, 3, and Penelope, 16 months.

And, much like Jen, Zac wants to make sure their children remain his and her priority.

"That's really my priorities," he shared. "I feel like probably the thing that's eating me alive the most is I feel like I failed my kids, and I'm worried that I'm going to cause them damage. And I just hope that we can still give them a normal childhood, but as far as that goes, Jen and I are definitely on the same page of wanting to give them the best the best life we can."

As for why their marriage came to an end, he reflected, “Marriage is hard. Having your marriage in the public is hard. Raising kids is hard. And I think slowly over time we lost that spark."

Meanwhile, for a closer look at where Jen and Zac’s SLOMW: OC costars stand in their love lives, read on. And don’t miss the premiere of The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County on Nov. 12.

Following her divorce from Cory Althoff, podcaster Bobbi Althoff is dating Tyler Hawkins, who plays a special role in Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County.

"He styles me, that’s how he supports me,” she told E! News in May, adding that the basketball player, "picks out my outfits every day. He styles me every day for the show. It’s great."

Aspyn Ovard, who filed for divorce from Parker Ferris in 2024 after eight years of marriage, is currently in a throuple relationship with married couple Billy Davis and Bri Davis.

After many ups and downs, Jen Affleck—who is pregnant with her and Zac Affleck's fourth baby—officially filed for divorce from her husband of seven years, E! News confirmed Nov. 4.

Despite ongoing relationship speculation, Salomé Andrea and Aaron Brimhall are still going strong as they prepare to welcome their third baby together. The duo, who married in 2012, also share 8-year-old twins Luca and Rue.

McCall DaPron—the sister of Mormon Wives' Mayci Neeley—shares four kids with husband Kirk DaPron, who she wed in 2011.

Ashleigh Pease and Nate Pease are rock solid after over a decade of marriage. "This 13th year together is one that has felt full circle!" she wrote on Instagram in September. "A season of fruits. Comfort. The happiest milestones."

Madison Bontempo and Kyler Steven Fisher, who wed in 2013, have been open about the highs and lows of their relationship. "Kyler and I aren’t perfect," she wrote on Instagram in October. "We’ve had arguments. We’ve had hard seasons. We’ve had moments we wish we could take back. And we’re not afraid to admit that."

But, she added, "Those moments aren’t our whole story. There’s also laughter, friendship, forgiveness, growth, five beautiful children, a million memories, and a love that has continued to evolve through every season of our lives."

Avery Woods and David Woods, parents to Ziggy and Stevie, are still feeling the love after a decade of marriage. As she simply put it, "my forever + always."

Google Pixel Tag Initial Review: The gold standard for Find Hub trackers, whatever that’s worth

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Google’s Find Hub had a tumultuous launch for its performance, but also for a lack of clear direction in hardware. Over two years later, that’s finally arriving in the Google Pixel Tag which, even just a few days in, is the obvious gold standard in the race to build an AirTag for Android devices.

There are plenty of really good Android Find Hub trackers on the market. Pebblebee and Chipolo have been long-standing examples of how to build good dual-network trackers that work on both Android and iOS, while Motorola’s Moto Tag series has delivered two Android-only trackers that deliver better performance and more focused features. But even with the Moto Tag, there’s a clear compromise. Features like the ability to ring your phone are tied to a separate app and, in turn, have some inherent clunkiness.

Pixel Tag is the device that finally brings it all together, and it’s brilliant so far.

I’ve only had the Pixel Tag for a week and, frankly, it’s impossible to properly review a tracker in that time. There are only so many ways to artificially test out how a tracker works, as most cases where you’ll find value in this sort of product can only occur in real life.

So, with that said, let’s focus on the fundamentals.

Google Pixel Tag pairs to your Android phone just like any other Find Hub tracker – though much faster compared to some others I’ve tested – but the differences are obvious towards the end of that process. The final screen shows options for setting ringer volume and an option to set up “Ring this phone.”

Those new features are what make the Pixel Tag so good, and so appealing compared to all of the competition. A quick double-tap on the Tag’s button rings whichever phone you connected it to, assuming it’s in range, and it works pretty reliably. You can also set up “left behind” alerts (now available on other Find Hub trackers too) for when the phone leaves the tracker behind – you can set up exclusion zones such as your home or work address.

One of my favorite examples of the added polish from Google is the ability to adjust the ringer volume on the page where you actually want to ring the tracker. In a crowded but quiet space where you know the tracker is close by, being able to quickly set the volume to low is a great addition. In my book, it’s the reason to buy Pixel Tag over any other Android tracker.

UWB, of course, only makes up-close finding all the better. I found this decently reliable on my Pixel 11 Pro XL, but it is a little bit frustrating that the directional cues vanish within about 10-12 feet. Fine for a backpack or something like that, potentially a problem if your Tag is attached to a set of keys.

On that note, my biggest hardware complaint is that Google took the Apple route of having no built-in mounting mechanism. The super thin and light hardware of Pixel Tag is wonderful, don’t get me wrong, but being forced to buy a separate accessory just to attach it to my bag is frustrating! One of Google’s partners, Bellroy, built one of these accessories and it’s simple enough – a silicone case with a rope. I wish I didn’t need it, but it works, and at least you get two of them in a pack for $25.

The hardware itself is pretty good across the board, really, with the battery door also being slightly easier to open compared to the Moto Tag. Inside is a standard CR2032 battery that Google says will last “over one year.” I’d personally still prefer a rechargeable battery with USB-C or wireless charging, but this is a good enough solution given the amount of time the battery lasts – hopefully Google’s claim lives up.

Rounding out some of the key aspects here, it’s worth saying that the built-in speaker is more than loud enough at its highest setting, but the value for me so far has really come in that the low setting is actually low enough to not be a nuisance. I do find it a little strange that there’s no visual indication of where the Tag’s button is, but the packaging and app both do a good enough job of letting you know.

Overall performance in Find Hub seems fine, but as mentioned at the outset, this is a really hard thing to test artificially, all the more so in the span of just a few days. Google’s Find Hub network has gotten a lot better in the past two years, but it’s still hit or miss in my experience. It’s very clear that Google is making an effort to reverse the privacy-inspired decision to effectively disable the Find Hub network outside of crowded areas, but the damage has been done. I’m not sure the network will ever fully recover from that misstep, regardless of what tracker you’re using it with.

I’m looking forward to seeing how Pixel Tag performs as the travel season spins back up, so stay tuned for a later long-term review of Google’s tracker in the coming months. Initial impressions, though, are solid. Sound off in the comments below if you have any questions around Pixel Tag, I’ll answer everything I can!

If you’re interested in picking up a Pixel Tag, it’ll be available on November 11.

Google Releases Modern Web Guidance 1.0 For AI Coding Agents via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

A free skill set for AI coding agents leaves preview with a codebase audit and public evals. One early test scored higher when agents were told to use it.

Google issued version 1.0 of Modern Web Guidance, which is a free skill set that web development teams may use as an enhancement to their AI coding tools, in order for those tools to write code based on current browser features. The skills are in the form of packaged guidelines that the agent will look at while writing the code. The code generated by the agent will also be sent to the same pages that are evaluated for metrics such as Largest Contentful Paint (LCP), Interaction to Next Paint (INP), and accessibility.

Philip Walton, a senior staff developer relations engineer at Google, announced the release in a Chrome for Developers post. Version 1.0 adds an audit for existing code, and Firefox and Edge are joining the project. Chrome first released the guidance as an early preview at Google I/O in May.

Teams can install it by running npx modern-web-guidance@latest install in a project directory. It’s also available through the plugin marketplaces of most popular coding agents, according to the post.

Version 1.0’s audit will go through a project’s HTML, CSS, JavaScript files as well as its package.json to identify areas where developers are able to safely update their code, then walk the agent through the changes. This first iteration of the audit uses static analysis to look at a limited number of code patterns, according to the post.

The post says the library now targets “hundreds of verified AI knowledge gaps” across its guides. Additionally, current coding models continue to fall short in Google’s tests of accessibility, security headers, and loading priority for scripts and images.

According to Google, each of the guides is now reviewed and approved by a domain tech lead. For user-facing feature guides there is a dedicated accessibility review done by experts who use assistive technologies daily.

A governance document was uploaded to the project’s GitHub repository on Nov. 9 which outlines six owner seats for the project. Two of those seats belong to Chrome, Microsoft Edge and Mozilla each have one, and two are given to independent web platform experts. There is currently one open independent seat and no seat belongs to Apple.

Google’s announcement links to a public eval dashboard where coding agents from Anthropic, OpenAI and Google run the same set of tasks every night, with and without the guidance. A real browser then checks the DOM, styles and source code of each finished app.

When I checked Tuesday morning, guided runs were passing 88% of the automated checks, whereas unguided runs were passing 56%. These figures are based upon each agent’s combined results for nightly runs completed Nov. 8; the range is 199 to 215 tasks per agent. The dashboard provides a limited review (not a full WCAG audit, it says) of accessibility features such as support for keyboards, focus, and form labels.

Its task prompts don’t mention the guidance, so each agent has to work out on its own when a guide applies. In most of the recent suites listed, guided runs ran slower and burned more tokens.

Accessibility consultant Adrian Roselli evaluated the preview in May by giving Antigravity (Google’s AI coding tool) an animated accordion prompt found in the documentation. However, according to Roselli, the output did not comply with WCAG and its animation was not functional in Firefox. He also found no assurance that agents would be able to follow the guidelines. This was for the preview, not v1.0.

The new guidelines were tested by Japan’s creator platform, note inc. using two coding agents that ran through six user interface (UI) tasks across 180 test runs on clone versions of note inc.’s production code base. As explained in a Chrome blog, both agents’ scores on 19 browser checks rose after installing the skill without naming it in prompts, then again once prompts named it. Agent One went from 60.8% to 76.5% to 94.0%. Similarly, Agent Two had scores of 75.8%, 86.2%, and 90.0% under the same three test conditions.

One of note’s tasks checked whether the largest image in view on an article page had high priority and wasn’t lazy-loaded. The comparison table in the post shows that unguided agents wrote hero image code using both high priority and lazy loading.

The guidance is run by MetaMask, as part of an automatic review process for each pull request submitted to its open-source browser extension, according to a second Chrome post. Through Oct. 21 (the first five weeks), the bot identified 102 issues across 57 feature and fix pull requests. The engineers fully resolved 43 of them and partially resolved three of them. Another 16 were either declined or false positives. Twenty of them went unaddressed. The outcome of the other 20 is unknown.

Modern Web Guidance provides a no-cost means for teams to encourage agents writing code to use native browser capabilities and that includes code in existing legacy code bases. Most of the results that have been published are based on testing conducted by Google as well as customer testing that Google highlighted. Note Inc. tested the two agents using the same prompt with and without telling them to use the guidance; they both performed better when the prompt did tell them to use the guidance. The team can also label their prompts to include the guidance and keep field data and manual accessibility testing in the review.

Google says it plans to expand this process of auditing existing code at some point, as well as performing audits in which the agent is reasoning about the actual code being audited.

The company suggests pairing the guidance with Chrome DevTools for agents to allow the agent to run its own created code and test it inside of the browser. Preview users can use the command npx modern-web-guidance@latest update.

Yellowstone's Downfall Can Be Traced Back To One Moment

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

There was a time when "Yellowstone" was on top of the world, so far as mainstream television was concerned. What started as a somewhat unassuming Western TV show on an upper-tier cable channel became a breakout hit, in no small part thanks to the participation of Oscar-winner Kevin Costner in the lead role as John Dutton. "Yellowstone" set a huge record as the most-watched show of 2022. It was massive. Then, it all came crashing down.

"Yellowstone" Season 5 became its last, with a massive gap between "Part 1" and "Part 2" of that season. Series creator Taylor Sheridan decided to focus more on the show's spin-offs, as well as his other projects, with the series finale airing in December 2024. That was that. But where, exactly, did it all go wrong? When was the downfall of this once mighty TV titan? Frankly, it's not that hard to figure out.

To put my cards on the table, I've not seen a single episode of the show. I'm merely someone who writes about this stuff professionally and pays attention to the business. Anyone who does that can tell you with confidence that the behind-the-scenes drama between Sheridan and Costner is without a doubt what doomed "Yellowstone." Once Costner checked out, however that actually went down, the writing was on the wall.

Indeed, it was reported in May 2023 that Costner was leaving "Yellowstone" after Season 5. That raised eyebrows and sounded alarms. Any time a lead actor leaves a show, that's to be expected. But as the headlines continued to swirl, it became clear that this wasn't just a guy stepping away from a series. There was much more to the story.

To this day, it's still not entirely clear how everything went down. What we know for sure is that Taylor Sheridan became the busiest man in showbusiness, with a ton of series on the air, all of which he was chiefly responsible for. Kevin Costner, meanwhile, had other projects he wanted to do while also fronting "Yellowstone." Tension mounted. It even came to light that Costner was "very upset" with "Yellowstone" Season 2, long before his departure.

The gap between the end of "Part 1" of "Yellowstone" Season 5 and "Part 2" is where things really started to go south. Costner wanted to go make his long-planned Western movie saga "Horizon," which he did. But that conflicted with the planned schedule for the show, which caused issues. Not for nothing, we still don't know what's going on with "Horizon: An American Saga — Chapter 2" after the first movie bombed in theaters. But that's another story.

Sheridan has explained Costner's surprising "Yellowstone" exit, suggesting that the actor stayed on the show even longer than he wanted to in the first place:

"Even though Kevin was ready to go —he had other things he wanted to do — he stayed on for another two seasons, and that was just because the show was such a behemoth and such a huge hit that the notion of giving up a hit before it had run of juice to squeeze is very foreign to a network."

But it's not that simple. Costner explained his "Yellowstone" exit and said he'd "probably go to court" over it in 2023. It wasn't an amicable parting of ways. The main point is that Costner leaving and the feud that went on undoubtedly derailed the show. It's that simple.

It's also worth pointing out that even when Kevin Costner was still on the show, things were far from rosey. Costner's clash with a co-star reportedly left "Yellowstone" lead Kelly Reilly in tears, and while Costner has a bit of a reputation for being difficult to work with in general, reports like this are still a bad look. At a certain point, even wildly popular TV shows are forced to buckle to external pressures, particularly when they involve their leading men.

Be that as it may, Taylor Sheridan and Paramount have made the most out of the situation. It's a real lemons/lemonade deal they've got going on. Spoilers, but "Yellowstone" Season 5 dealt with John Dutton by revealing he's dead. The show moved on and got to its finale without him. More than that, Sheridan has made the most of the greater "Yellowstone" universe he established by not only developing prequels such as "1883" and "1923" but spin-offs as well.

First and foremost is "Dutton Ranch," which is getting a Season 2. What happens beyond that remains to be seen, but its protagonists Beth Dutton (Reilly) and Rip Wheeler (Cole Hauser) have effectively picked up where John Dutton left off. There's also "Marshals," which has brought Luke Grimes' Kayce Dutton to network TV, resulting in a big hit on CBS. So, even without Costner, the "Yellowstone" franchise has thrived. It wasn't a happy marriage or a happy divorce, but the dust settled, and everyone figured out a path forward.

Oscar Contender ‘Bucks Harbor’ Set To Begin Awards-Qualifying Run; Watch Clip From Pete Muller’s Impressive Directorial Debut

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: Bucks Harbor, one of the most acclaimed documentaries of the year, is about to begin its Oscar-qualifying theatrical run.

The film directed by Pete Muller opens at Firehouse: DCTV’s Cinema for Documentary Film in Manhattan on December 4. The feature documentary set in a remote coastal village of Northern Maine, where generations of men have stoically fished the waters in an often-hazardous pursuit of a living, premiered in February at the Berlin Film Festival. At the Berlinale, it won the Prize of the Ecumenical Jury and the Audience Award in the festival’s Panorama section. Bucks Harbor also won Best International Documentary Film at the Zurich Film Festival, Best Documentary Film at the Tacoma Film Festival in Washington State, the Audience Award at the Margaret Mead Film Festival in New York, and the Fowlie Audience Award at the Camden International Film Festival in Maine, among many other awards. Related Stories Casting Millie Bobby Brown To Star In, Produce Netflix Comedy 'Non-Refundable'; Jack Innanen Also Set News Female-Led Action-Thriller 'Topple' Launches With Carla Diaz, Maria Pedraza, Tamara Feldman & Graham McTavish Among Cast -- AFM

Bucks Harbor screens at DOC NYC on Sunday after being named to that festival’s Winners Circle for 2026. Watch an exclusive clip from the film below.

“Set against the rugged beauty of Downeast Maine, the film portrays a lobster fishing community shaped by the unforgiving coastal landscape,” notes a synopsis. “As Bucks Harbor delicately explores working class manhood, kinship and the act of listening, it situates human life alongside the ancient creatures that sustain it, offering a revelatory, searing and ultimately hopeful portrait.”

The documentary features several generations of indelible individuals in Bucks Harbor: David Cale, Vicki Patchell, Wayne Robinson, Mark Alley, Sharon Alley, Larson Alley, Mike Murphy, Kirby Murphy, Carter Murphy, Ryan Murphy, and Isaac Reynolds.

Muller, who makes his directorial debut with Bucks Harbor, won an Emmy as co-producer of The First Wave. He’s an award-winning photographer who spent more than a decade documenting conflict in post-colonial societies around the world. Muller developed his interest in photography as a youth after accompanying his mother while she worked night shifts as a newspaper photographer in Lynn, MA.

“[W]e’d race to fires, shootings, and other emergencies that crackled through the police scanner,” Muller writes in a director’s statement. “From the safety of the company car, where she insisted I remain, I’d watch her move through the flashing red and blue lights of bad situations. At the end of the night, in the darkroom, I’d ask questions about what we’d seen and why. Her answers were shaped by compassion and became the basis of my education about the world.

“I nourished my interest in people by studying history but learned I wasn’t built to sit quietly and read books. Visual journalism seemed a better fit as it rewarded my desire to be so close that I’d be shoved and splattered. For nearly 15 years, I worked as a photojournalist in Africa and the Middle East, seeking to understand and communicate the causes of armed conflict. During that time, I came to see ideas of masculinity as a central feature of the conflicts I witnessed.”

Muller continues, “In 2021, I returned to New England and began what would become Bucks Harbor. I spent the next five years in winding, intimate conversations with the film’s participants, who came to feel like extended family. Each felt deeply familiar, reminding me of relatives, close friends, and myself. While Bucks Harbor is built around their lives, it reflects mine, too.

“I’ve long interrogated the lessons we were taught as boys: be tough and strong, don’t accept humiliation, don’t express fear or insecurity. What did those lessons prepare us for? What do our families, friends, and societies truly want and need from us? And most importantly, how do we feel beneath the stoic exteriors we were encouraged to assume?”

Muller adds that his documentary “is not solely an exploration of men and masculinity. While men and boys offer central perspectives, women contribute vital, illuminating insights along the way. The film is about survival, love, and the emotional armor we all develop. It’s also about the experience of working people in the United States and the quiet courage and fortitude required just to get by. And it explores our abiding relationship with the natural world and the wisdom it offers if we’re willing to listen. I hope Bucks Harbor can serve as a constructive, humanistic tile in the mosaic of our social conversation and reflect my fundamental belief that we need each other. The more we can relate and empathize, the closer we might move toward shared understanding.”

Bucks Harbor is directed and produced by Pete Muller and produced by Nathan Golon and Noel Paul. The cinematography is by Nathan Golon and Pete Muller. Lobster cinematography is by Mark Unger, Anand Varma, and Pete Muller. Noel Paul edited the film. Nikolaj Hess composed the original score; the film also features an original composition by Hiss Golden Messenger. Executive producers are Kaitlin Yarnall and Greg Moga.

In the exclusive clip below, Bucks Harbor’s David Cale fishes from a dock with his cousin’s son, Isaac Reynolds.

GdS: Tactics, data and team-mates – the ‘strange case’ of Goncalo Ramos at Milan

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

The Goncalo Ramos enigma continues to dominate the newspaper columns, as AC Milan try unlock their record signing.

As La Gazzetta dello Sport report, Milan fans hoped they had finally found a top-class striker in Ramos, but his three goals this season are not sufficient compared to expectations and the €70m price tag for which he arrived.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

And yet, the Portuguese centre-forward is still proving to be a factor in the team’s fortunes. The reason for this lies behind statistics that the average fan might ignore, but Ruben Amorim certainly isn’t.

The coach has repeatedly emphasised how Ramos is helping the team, less so the other way around. Looking at the numbers, you can’t argue with him. Gonçalo may not be making it into the top scorers’ table, but his work is well explained by statistics that place him among the top strikers in Serie A.

Which ones? Passes (20.7 per game, first in volume among centre-forwards in the league ahead of Lazio’s Noslin) or touches (2nd), for example. But also the aerial duels won and fouls drawn (19, first among attackers) underscore the importance of his sacrifice too.

There are two considerations that don’t emerge from the statistics, but are equally important. The first concerns the movements that free up or facilitate team-mates, such as the sprint for Milan’s first goal in Genoa.

Alphadjo Cisse found himself all alone in the penalty area thanks to Ramos drawing two of the Rossoblu central defenders towards him, allowing Adrien Rabiot to easily pass to the young Italian who was in lots of space.

The second consideration concerns the pressure and coverage work, which has changed since the start of the season. In the early days, Gonçalo was the first to apply pressure up front, but in doing so, he exposed Modric to too much space to cover in front of or behind him.

Amorim therefore asked the Portuguese to instead harass the opposing playmaker, without attacking the defence in the initial possession. This move allows Luka to run less and think more during the opponent’s possession phase.

We were talking about a Milan side that is helping Ramos less than the centre-forward is helping the team, too. The former Benfica man is 19th in Serie A for shots (26) and 15th for shots on target (10).

While it’s true that the Rossoneri maintain a high standard in terms of the quality of chances created (third in the league for big chances behind Inter and Roma), it’s equally undeniable that they arrive in the final third of the pitch less often than expected. Milan are 12th for touches in the opponent’s penalty area and 12th for shots on target.

In short, Ramos sees the ball mostly where he can’t hurt the goalkeepers. Even if, in relation to goal-scoring chances, he should have scored double in the league: 4.16 xG compared to the just two scored. Still, not enough for a Milan-calibre player.

Messina Touring Group Looks To The Future: ‘As Things Are Changing, We Adapt’

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The company's president of Nashville Haley McCollister reflects on Louis Messina's mentorship and belief in his staff.

The only job Haley McCollister has ever had has been at Messina Touring Group. Since joining the company’s ticketing department at 22 years old, she’s worked her way up and now serves as president of MTG’s Nashville office.

“The more he believed in me, the more I believed in myself,” McCollister says.

McCollister notes that many of her MTG colleagues have been with the company since they started their first jobs out of college. This is due to company founder Louis Messina’s strong belief in each employee, with McCollister likening his support to how he strengthens his relationships with his artists.

Aside from McCollister, key members of the next generation at MTG include senior vp Sara Winter, vp of touring Keena Cheatham and vp Rachel Powers.

As the company continues to support its promoters, Messina has been passing more responsibilities onto his fellow teammates and providing them opportunities to foster their own artists. MTG maintains a small artist roster, wanting to provide all its attention to each client rather than having each person focus on multiple acts.

“We’re not out there chasing everything,” McCollister says. “To keep it this way, we have to grow very slowly. To find the ‘right’ artist for us to grow with starts with someone being extremely passionate about them and feeling it’s the right fit. And Louis is really supportive of us.”

McCollister and her colleagues fell in love with Phoebe Bridgers while she was opening for Taylor Swift on The Eras Tour and began discussions about bringing her into the fold. Messina came on board, and Bridgers is now promoted by MTG.

As the company looks ahead to its next quarter-­century, the team members feel they’re operating in a way they hope to be able to sustain.

“For the next 25 years, it’s going to look similar to what we’ve already done,” McCollister says. “As the business continues to change, that doesn’t impact how we operate, because we’ve always operated unique to each individual artist. As things are changing, we adapt with them and are continuing to deliver long-term success.”

This story appears in the Nov. 7, 2026, issue of Billboard.

Do this crucial thing to thrive during unemployment

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A job search, whatever the length, is demoralizing.

After losing a job, you’ll start asking yourself challenging questions about finances, childcare and more. You may also feel the pang of an identity crisis—particularly if you’re a longtime employee whose daily life has become tied up with work.

On top of that, there’s the hours of applying, networking, interviewing, and dealing with modern-day job hunt maladies, from AI and ghost jobs. Lack of communication and recruiter bait-and-switch make the process feel useless. 

The mental battle can be a monster. But experts suggest a possible sanity-saving sword to slay that monster: the power of having a routine. That can be key, especially when navigating a process that may drag on past six months or more.

Melissa Dorman, an organizational psychologist and executive coach, shares that in today’s anonymous market, “no matter how much you get done, you can feel behind or get sucked into the vortex of applying for one job after another.”

Crafting (and sticking) to a routine during this period is not only a way to stay positive during a job search—but it could also be a means to open up job seekers to new opportunities.

Routines and productivity

The salve to some of this strangeness may come down to how seekers control what is within their power. A routine can serve as that support and stability that can put a period of uncontrollable unpredictability into perspective. 

“Stability is everything when your world feels like the bottom has fallen out, especially when losing a job and searching for a job in a volatile market,” says Dorman.

By setting routines, what matters is, “you can know how your day will start tomorrow morning.”

As Dorman recommends, try to set specific times for job applications. Outside of that time, focus on developing new skills, conducting networking outreach, or simply giving yourself some grace to reconnect with what brings you joy. These new activities can not only bring you joy—but serve as anchors throughout your day. Over time, you may see that your routine gives you purpose.

“One sanity-protecting exercise is to implement an ‘office hours’ approach for apps,” adds Dorman, who frequently coaches managers on keeping their skills sharp. 

With these kinds of consistent routines, you can free up mental space so you are able to meet the perfect opportunity when it presents itself: “You can’t be productive or maintain motivation long-term if [only] completing job applications is your full-time job.”

Benefits of consistency

Routines can be tricky to make stick, though—the human brain is wired to feel lit up by novel experiences.

“Our brains love new things. When we start a routine, we get a bit of a dopamine hit, or a burst of the motivational chemical that craves rewards. But when the routine becomes familiar, what once felt exciting starts to feel like work,” says Dorman.

That’s why being intentionally consistent is key: blocking off time on your calendar for that 4:30 p.m. jog, or booking a hot desk at your favorite coworking space, where you specifically research interesting companies or dig into building new skills.

With repetition, not only does the craving for novelty become less pronounced, but the routine itself starts to reinforce itself from positive associations. Your weekly workout may suddenly be the most significant time to relax and refresh yourself, for example.

Make time in the first few weeks to feel a little uncomfortable when cementing your new routine—but stick to a regular schedule. And if you’re comfortable sharing your new routine, recruit a close friend to keep you accountable through regular check-ins.

“The more often you do something, the less you have to think about it,” says Dorman.

Being consistent also makes us feel more productive. According to long-cited research by Teresa Amabile, a psychologist and professor emerita at Harvard Business School, the act of making progress on a project of any size involving personal investment leads to feelings of productivity. Her team dubbed this concept the “progress principle.” Amabile’s research shows this ability to make even a small dent on a project improves workers’ motivation, emotional health, and sense of personal performance. The frequency of these feelings also affected the duration subjects remained productive.

In essence, building up wins throughout the day equaled a feeling of motivation for people over the long-term.

Crafting your successful routine

  • 1. Make it a habit


Habits are borne from planning ahead. One place to start when you’re still looking for work? Take an audit of your sleep schedule. Laura Vanderkam, a Fast Company contributor who has written about productive scheduling, stresses the importance of a consistent sleep schedule in her 2022 book, Tranquility by Tuesday.

Unorganized sleep schedules are hidden saboteurs, causing people to crash out and squander productivity in order to make up for rest. It’s too easy to get into a cycle of stretching yourself thin during the work week—only to oversleep on weekends and throw off your rhythm on weekdays. With a consistent sleep and wake-up time, you not only regulate your flow of energy for the day, but you’re setting yourself up for success tomorrow on your terms.

  • 2. Place rewards alongside hard work

Harvard behavioral scientists Katy Milkman, Julia Minson, and Kevin Volpp point to the practice of bundling temptations alongside tedious or daunting work to fuel motivation. Pair activities that are a chore or bore you with a motivational pick-me-up—like your favorite coffee or a few minutes of vinyasa flow.

And Thomas Davies, a previous director at Google for Work and Fast Company contributor, writes that engaging in activities that genuinely light you up will make an onerous or soulless job search feel more fruitful or at least seem like one step to something better.

MIT researchers also draw a connection between the importance of mental hints when developing habits. They claim habits are often associated with a physical cue that triggers a habit response—so go ahead and reach for your noise-cancelling headphones or shift over to your standing desk when hard work calls.

  • 3. Don’t ignore the paths of least resistance

Finally, keeping a routine doesn’t mean being inflexible.

According to a 2026 study carried out by Columbia researchers, people often get stuck in inertia (fixed mindsets, anyone?), which can cause them to disregard new pathways to their goal. Sometimes, the more interesting route can be more fun and lead you to the same result. So stay open to changing your set routines when it feels right. Maybe this means changing the ratio of your networking efforts to application efforts. Maybe this means searching for skills over strict job titles. 

Whatever the shift looks like, the idea applies to before you get the job… and when you land it.

Keep these tips to make your routine a steady one—and to make your unemployment period a productive one.
